# Combination catalogue

Each identifier selects a distinct member of the authoring space. The rows follow the game’s public catalogues and the mission questions in the current content pack. All 1,200 supplied micro-skills have a searchable public description and retain their worksheet number. A word and its history are independent selections.

[Combination rules, worked journeys and lookup commands](COMBINATION_ATLAS.md)

<a id="family-etymology"></a>

## Word histories

| ID | Member | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `etymology:trace` | Trace | Middle English could use trace for a track. It came through Old French, where it could also follow a course or draw a line. [Source](https://www.ahdictionary.com/word/search.html?q=trace) |
| `etymology:symmetry` | Symmetry | The word passed through Latin from Greek. Its Greek parts bring together and measure into the same word. [Source](https://www.ahdictionary.com/word/search.html?q=symmetry) |
| `etymology:truth` | Truth | Old English treowth carried a sense of faithfulness. Its descendant trewthe was used in Middle English. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/truth) |
| `etymology:power` | Power | The Anglo-French poer could describe being able to do something. The English noun power grew out of that verb. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/power) |
| `etymology:explanation` | Explanation | The verb explain goes back to Latin explanare, to make level. Its relative planus meant flat. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/explain) |
| `etymology:culture` | Culture | Culture came through French from Latin cultura. In Middle English it could refer to cultivated ground or the work of tending it. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/culture) |
| `etymology:values` | Values | The Middle English word for worth came through French. Farther back, Latin valere could mean to be strong or well. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/value) |
| `etymology:responsibility` | Responsibility | In this word family, responsible came from Anglo-French responsable. The older word is related to an answer or response. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/responsible) |
| `etymology:evidence` | Evidence | Latin videre meant to see. It sits behind evidentia, something plain to view. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/evidence) |
| `etymology:claim` | Claim | Anglo-French clamer could mean asking for something as a right. Its Latin ancestor clamare meant to call out. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/claim) |
| `etymology:justification` | Justification | Follow the verb justify back through Middle English justifien to Late Latin justificare, a relative of justus, meaning just. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/justify) |
| `etymology:certainty` | Certainty | Middle English speakers used certeinte. They borrowed it from Anglo-French certeinté, built from the word for certain. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/certainty) |
| `etymology:interpretation` | Interpretation | The verb interpret leads back to Latin interpres. An interpres could be an interpreter, an agent or a negotiator. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/interpret) |
| `etymology:perspective` | Perspective | The word has several histories of looking. A Middle English form for an optical device goes back to Latin perspicere, to look through. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/perspective) |
| `etymology:objectivity` | Objectivity | Objective traces back to Medieval Latin objectivus and objectum, something presented to the mind. English formed objectivity from objective. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/objective) |
| `etymology:source` | Source | A surse in Anglo-French could be a spring. Its verb meant to rise, like water coming out of the ground. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/source) |
| `etymology:criterion` | Criterion | Greek kritērion was a means of judging. It belongs to the same word family as krinein, to separate or decide. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/criterion) |
| `etymology:counterclaim` | Counterclaim | The counter- in counterclaim came by way of French. Its Latin ancestor contra meant against or facing. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/counter) |
| `etymology:provenance` | Provenance | French provenir means to come from. Further back are two Latin parts meaning forth and come. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/provenance) |
| `etymology:documented` | Documented | Document comes from Latin documentum. It could mean a lesson or a piece of proof. The related verb docere meant to teach. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/document) |
| `etymology:reconstruction` | Reconstruction | Look inside reconstruction for construct. Its Latin relative construere was formed with struere, to build. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/construct) |
| `etymology:simulation` | Simulation | Latin simulatio could mean imitation. Middle English used simulacioun for pretence. A workshop simulation gives that old idea of imitation a different job. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/simulation) |
| `etymology:patent` | Patent | Latin patere meant to stand open. It supplied the word that came through French into English as patent. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/patent) |
| `etymology:bias` | Bias | English borrowed bias from Middle French biais. One early image associated with the word is a slant. [Source](https://ahdictionary.com/word/search.html?q=bias) |
| `etymology:knowledge-question` | Knowledge question | The question in this phrase came through French from Latin quaestio. Its verb quaerere meant to seek or ask. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/question) |
| `etymology:research-question` | Research question | The research in this phrase came through French recerche. The related verb described going about in search of something. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/research) |
| `etymology:deduction` | Deduction | The word reached English through French and from Latin deductio. The Latin word could describe leading something away or making an amount smaller. The English reasoning sense concerns what follows from a starting point. [Source](https://www.etymonline.com/word/deduction) |
| `etymology:induction` | Induction | Latin inductio could mean a leading in or an introduction. Cicero used it to translate a Greek term for reasoning from cases. [Source](https://www.etymonline.com/word/induction) |
| `etymology:abduction` | Abduction | The Latin verb abducere meant to lead or take away. English has several uses of abduction. Its reasoning sense names a later technical use. [Source](https://www.etymonline.com/word/abduction) |
| `etymology:analogy` | Analogy | Analogy reached Middle English through French and Latin. Its Greek roots expressed proportion or correspondence between things. [Source](https://www.ahdictionary.com/word/search.html?q=analogy) |
| `etymology:causal` | Causal reasoning | The English adjective came from Late Latin causalis. The Latin form was built from causa, a word for a cause or reason. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/causal) |
| `etymology:pragmatic` | Pragmatic reasoning | Greek pragma meant a deed. The word came through Latin, where its form could describe being skilled in practical business. [Source](https://www.ahdictionary.com/word/search.html?q=pragmatic) |
| `etymology:concession` | Concession | Middle English took the noun through Anglo-French. The Latin source belonged to a family of words for giving way or granting a point. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/concession) |
| `etymology:reframe` | Reframe | English made reframe from re-, meaning again, and the verb frame. An early use described putting something together anew. [Source](https://www.etymonline.com/word/reframe) |
| `etymology:model` | Model | English took model from French modelle. Its Italian ancestor modello goes back to Latin modulus, a small measure. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/model) |
| `etymology:context` | Context | Latin contexere meant to weave together. Contextus could describe a connection or the way words fit together before context took on its wider English sense. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/context) |
| `etymology:measurement` | Measurement | Middle English mesurement came from an Anglo-French word for the act of measuring. It was also used for the apportioning of shares. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/measurement) |
| `etymology:hypothesis` | Hypothesis | Greek hypotithenai could mean to put under or to suppose. Hypothesis comes from that idea of laying down a supposition. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/hypothesis) |
| `etymology:correlation` | Correlation | Medieval Latin correlatio joined a form of com-, together, to relatio, a relation. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/correlation) |
| `etymology:slip` | Slip | The paper sense of slip may come from Middle Dutch or Middle Low German slippe, a strip or slit cut from something. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/slip) |
| `etymology:docket` | Docket | Middle English had doggette, a short note that summed up a longer document. Where the word came from before that is not certain. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/docket) |
| `etymology:tally` | Tally | Anglo-French tallie came from Latin talea, a twig or cutting. People once cut notches in a stick to keep a count. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/tally) |
| `etymology:ledger` | Ledger | Middle English legger probably comes from leggen, to lay. A ledger was a large book that lay in one place. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/ledger) |
| `etymology:custody` | Custody | Latin custodia meant guarding. It comes from custos, a guard or keeper. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/custody) |
| `etymology:testimony` | Testimony | Latin testimonium comes from testis, a witness. Testimony is what a witness says. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/testimony) |
| `etymology:attribution` | Attribution | Latin attribuere meant to assign or give to someone. Its parts are ad, to, and tribuere, to allot. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/attribute) |
| `etymology:accession` | Accession | Latin accessio was an addition. Its verb accedere meant to come near or to be added. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/accession) |
| `etymology:version` | Version | Latin vertere meant to turn. Medieval Latin versio named a turning or change, and the word passed through French. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/version) |
| `etymology:gloss` | Gloss | The gloss at the edge of a page traces back to Greek glossa. That word could mean a tongue, a language or a word that needed explaining. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/gloss#h3) |
| `etymology:witness` | Witness | Old English witnes could mean knowledge or testimony. It gives us the modern word for someone who can speak from experience. [Source](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/witness) |
| `etymology:clue` | Clue | A clew was a ball of thread. The story of Theseus following a thread through a maze helped give clue its sense of a guide. [Source](https://www.ahdictionary.com/word/search.html?q=clue) |

<a id="family-exhibition"></a>

## Exhibition prompt families

| ID | Member | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `exhibition:01` | Prompt 1 · What can I put on a public label and expect the next person to check? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:02` | Prompt 2 · Which information saves the next keeper a wasted trip? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:03` | Prompt 3 · When is it safe to act on a record I did not make? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:04` | Prompt 4 · What detail should make me pause before carrying the usual story onward? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:05` | Prompt 5 · What could settle the point these two records disagree about? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:06` | Prompt 6 · What will I find later if I file this record under that heading? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:07` | Prompt 7 · Whose next move changes when this becomes the public version? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:08` | Prompt 8 · When can I make a firm promise from a test? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:09` | Prompt 9 · What could make two readers see different things in the same mark? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:10` | Prompt 10 · How can a message stay useful after it leaves its first reader? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:11` | Prompt 11 · What would let someone who acted on the first notice change their mind? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:12` | Prompt 12 · Whose experience slips out of the picture when I count from here? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:13` | Prompt 13 · How could a later keeper tell which part of the record improved? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:14` | Prompt 14 · Which people can make sense of the name a map carries? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:15` | Prompt 15 · What still cannot be checked from the place where I stand? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:16` | Prompt 16 · When should a keeper leave a true detail out of the public copy? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:17` | Prompt 17 · Who might benefit from doing the work to check the first story? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:18` | Prompt 18 · What should stay open even after both sources have been read? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:19` | Prompt 19 · Which part of my reason would survive if the card went missing? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:20` | Prompt 20 · What changed when I tried the other person’s vantage point? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:21` | Prompt 21 · What gets lost if I use one heading for practices from different places? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:22` | Prompt 22 · Where does the specialist’s check end and someone else’s work begin? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:23` | Prompt 23 · What part of the situation can this instrument make visible? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:24` | Prompt 24 · Why did a notice work in one place and miss people in another? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:25` | Prompt 25 · Which part did I observe and which part am I prepared to infer? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:26` | Prompt 26 · What could our crew find together that each of us would miss? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:27` | Prompt 27 · What do I owe the person who may act on the evidence I send? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:28` | Prompt 28 · Which parts of a result would stay the same if someone else ran the check? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:29` | Prompt 29 · Who has the authority to file, release, or pass on this account? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:30` | Prompt 30 · What can a model or an imagined scene help us notice about the real case? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:31` | Prompt 31 · What would make this evidence enough to take the next step? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:32` | Prompt 32 · What should a useful explanation let someone do here? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:33` | Prompt 33 · What earlier filing decision is still shaping what I find today? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:34` | Prompt 34 · What would I miss if I only looked for the result I wanted? | Scene-specific adaptation |
| `exhibition:35` | Prompt 35 · Whose priorities did we build into this rule or performance? | Scene-specific adaptation |
